                 Universality of critical quench autocorrelations
                     in the Sherrington–Kirkpatrick model
                                                  OpenAI

                                             October 5, 2026


                                                  Abstract
          We prove joint functional convergence of the stationary and quench autocorrelations of
      zero-field Sherrington–Kirkpatrick heat-bath dynamics at inverse temperature β = 1, with the
      same random limit for Gaussian and Rademacher couplings. Each site has a rate-one clock,
      mean spin autocorrelations are multiplied by n1/3 , and waiting times and lags are measured
      in units n2/3 . The quench starts from independent fair spins, and convergence is uniform on
      compact sets of positive waiting times and lags. The quench limit is selected by these initial
      states and relaxes to the stationary limiting autocorrelation as the waiting time tends to infinity.

1     Introduction
A quench starts a spin system from a simple nonequilibrium law and then lets it evolve at a
prescribed temperature. At the critical temperature of the Sherrington–Kirkpatrick model, the
relevant relaxation time grows with the number of spins. We determine the two-time autocorrelation
after a quench from independent symmetric spins. The limit retains the random spectral edge of the
interaction matrix and is governed by the same diffusion as the stationary critical autocorrelation.

1.1   The model and the result
Let D be either the standard Gaussian law or the uniform law on {−1, 1}. For n ≥ 2, let Jij , i < j,
be independent with law D, and put Jji = Jij , Jii = 0. The critical zero-field Gibbs measure is

The positive-time density limits of the Gaussian dynamics with initial law νn select this family, as
made precise in Theorem 6.1. The normalization is exactly (1.2)–(1.3).

   The boundary δ0 is interpreted in the product topology. At small times each fixed edge coordinate
can vanish while the renormalized constraint is carried by coordinates whose indices tend to infinity.
The proof identifies which such entrance is selected by the uniform starts through a stronger,
quantitative condition on these coordinates.

1.2   History and significance
Sherrington and Kirkpatrick introduced the infinite-range spin-glass model in 1975 [24]. The
subsequent dynamical theory studied memory through two-time correlation and response functions.
In particular, Sompolinsky and Zippelius developed a dynamical mean-field treatment for soft spins
[25], and Cugliandolo and Kurchan analyzed nonequilibrium relaxation after a quench in a soft-spin
Langevin formulation [9]. Their asymptotic regime takes the system-size limit before the long-time
limit and differs from the finite-size critical window considered here. For critical Ising heat-bath
dynamics, Billoire and Campbell [3, Equation (7)] numerically tested the stationary finite-size form

                                      qc (n, t) ∼ n−1/3 F (t/n2/3 ),

with time measured in updates per spin. Theorem 1.1 studies the two positive times following a
uniform quench and identifies the law of the sample-dependent limiting function.
    The spectral mechanism is especially transparent in spherical spin glasses. Kosterlitz, Thouless,
and Jones [17] analyzed the infinite-range Gaussian spherical spin glass using properties of large

                                                       4
random matrices. Cugliandolo and Dean [8] studied relaxation and aging for the spherical model,
while Ben Arous, Dembo, and Guionnet [2] proved limiting dynamical and aging results with a soft
spherical constraint. Fyodorov, Perret, and Schehr [14] later connected finite-size randomness on
the n2/3 scale to the edge in zero-temperature spherical dynamics. These works concern different
spin spaces or temperature regimes; they explain why the critical edge should remain visible in a
nonequilibrium limit.
    The stationary critical Ising theorem [22] identifies the conditioned edge measure, its closed
dynamical form, and the microscopic coefficient c∗ . Its equilibrium comparison uses the conditional
eigenframe averaging of Comets [7, Equations (2.1)–(2.2)], which equates the Haar-averaged cube and
spherical partition integrals when both use normalized reference measures and the same quadratic
Hamiltonian, and the critical conditioned-Gaussian description of Du and Huang [10, 11]. Using the
cap and spectral estimates of [22, 20, 21], Sections 3 and 4 extend cap control down to a tight lower
threshold for np3 and derive warming uniformly over the initial law at each positive critical time.
The order-n entropy argument uses Lemma 4.5, which strengthens the square-density posterior-mean
estimate of [19, Proposition 7.3] to square-root dependence on the relative heat-bath energy. The
stationary relaxed-energy theorem is applied within its polynomially bounded test class through
bounded resolvent optimizers in Section 5.

1.3   The argument
In the Gaussian model, adding an independent GOE diagonal changes the spin Hamiltonian only by
a constant. Let ua be orthonormal eigenvectors of the completed matrix and put xa = n−1/3 ⟨ua , σ⟩.
If ws is the Gibbs-relative density at time sTn , orthogonality rewrites the quench correlation as
                                                 n
                                                 X
                                                                    J
                                 Bn,J (s, t) =         πn,J [ws xa PtT x ].
                                                                      n a
                                                 a=1

This formula separates the law reached after the quench from subsequent relaxation. The source
identifies the relaxation operator for sufficiently controlled tests. Three further arguments are
needed.
    We first regularize the law reached after the quench. At scale p, a nested Gaussian observation
gives noisy information about the spin projections onto modes with 2 − λa ≤ p2 ; conditioning
caps those eigenvalues at 2 − p2 . On the retained spectral events, their number is comparable to
kp = np3 . Section 3 bounds the variance left unresolved for 0 ≤ f ≤ 1 by Cp−2 times the heat-bath
                   a
energy plus Ce−ckp , down to a fixed threshold for kp at each prescribed disorder confidence. In the
small-entropy part of Section 4, a contradiction argument assumes that a bounded level test f has
less energy than the claimed profile bound. At a later scale R, this makes the normalized tilt by f 2
close to the normalized observation tilt by FR2 , where FR = E[f | hR ]. Following its entropy through
earlier observations, together with the source path to a product posterior at higher entropy levels,
yields entropy and kernel bounds at every fixed positive critical time, uniformly over the initial law.
    For bounded cylinder sources, the finite Markov resolvent contraction bounds the optimizers
used in the stationary relaxed-energy theorem. Section 5 derives strong semigroup convergence
from that theorem and combines it with the warming bounds on densities and transition kernels.
This produces positive-time families on represented Gaussian subsequences, evolving by Stg , which
may initially retain randomness beyond g. To select one, choose a measurable admissible b with
Dg (b) > 0, put ri = gi + b, and define
                                                           X x2
                                                                i
                                          Mg (x) =                   .
                                                               2ri
                                                           i


                                                       5
We prove that 0 < Mg (x) < ∞ for Πg -almost every x and therefore may define, on that full-measure
set, the probability weights
                                                    x2i
                                       pi (x) =            .
                                                2ri Mg (x)
For each represented Gaussian limit, the averaged uniform-start estimate yields a deterministic
sequence sk ↓ 0 along which these weights leave every fixed finite set in probability under hsk Πg ,
almost surely in the represented data. Section 6 considers semigroup families whose densities are
locally uniformly bounded in L∞ (Πg ) and satisfy such sequential escape. Angular ratios and a
common rescaling give linear mode equations driven by independent Brownian motions with a
common diffusion factor. The square constraint gives a scalar Volterra equation whose high-mode
responses approach a common kernel. A stopped coupling proves fixed-g uniqueness and the full
s ↓ 0 product-topology boundary. The proof then constructs the common family measurably from g.
    Finally, we compare smooth functions of the stationary and quench traces under entry replacement
                                                                               √
[6]. An order-k derivative with respect to the normalized interaction Jij / n is accompanied by
n−k/2 , while summing over entries contributes order n2 . The two coupling laws match through
order three, so the averaged fourth derivative must gain a vanishing factor. Sections 7–9 first impose
static overlap-tail estimates for every interaction matrix in a cutoff’s support. Under the associated
normalized weighted path laws, these estimates yield history-overlap bounds with failure probability
O(n−A ) for any prescribed A > 0, after the cutoff parameters are chosen. Forward and reverse
conditional estimates for endpoint-normalized likelihoods on a binary tree of transition intervals
then control the derivatives over the full critical time span. The estimates also include derivatives
of the cutoff weights. They transfer the joint trace laws and the probability of retaining the cutoffs
to Rademacher disorder; smooth grid tests then transfer tightness in the locally uniform topology.
Section 10 assembles the result.


2     The stationary edge input
We fix the finite normalization and record the stationary edge, static-comparison, and relaxed-energy
inputs used below. Source cap estimates are stated at their points of use in Section 3; the following
sections develop the extensions, entrance construction, and disorder comparison.

2.2    Conditioned measure and relaxed energy
The following statements are the parts of the stationary manuscript that we use directly. They also
specify the meaning of the conditional measure in the introduction. A smooth compact cylinder is a
smooth function of finitely many coordinates, compactly supported in those coordinates.
Proposition 2.1 (Stationary inputs). The following hold for the edge law and the Gaussian finite
models.

     usual contraction rule for gradients passes to the closure, so the form is Dirichlet and Stg is a
     conservative symmetric Markov semigroup.

bound; the strong resolvent convergence needed here is derived in Section 5.

2.3    High-probability estimates and subsequences
We make one convention for random constants. An estimate holds with tight constants if, for every
χ > 0, there are deterministic constants in that estimate and events Gn,χ such that

                                        lim inf P(Gn,χ ) ≥ 1 − χ
                                         n→∞



                                                    8
and the estimate holds on Gn,χ simultaneously for every scale and test function in its stated class.
The constants may depend on χ and on fixed parameters specified before the estimate, but not on
n, the scale, or the test. A tight lower threshold for the dimension parameter kp = np3 has the same
meaning with a deterministic threshold Kχ on each event. When an estimate is required above
a vanishing time threshold, this means that for each χ a deterministic threshold εn,χ ↓ 0 can be
chosen on those events.
    We use the represented-subsequence convention of [22, Remark 2.2]. The edge points, the count-
ably many cylinder integrals and error quantities in use, and bounds that are tight in the preceding
sense are included in a joint law before applying the subsequence and Skorokhod representation
theorems. A further diagonal subsequence handles an iterated limit such as limm→∞ lim supn . On
the resulting probability space, the relevant bounds are finite and the chosen coordinates converge
almost surely. Conditional laws of the remaining finite data recover the original finite models.
This construction is used only to show that every subsequence has a further subsequence with the
identified limit, which implies the asserted convergence in law.
    For a disorder-dependent test, we use the separate class-uniform quenched convention of [22,
Remark 2.2]: each invoked inequality holds on one event for every test in its stated norm, energy,
and polynomial-growth class. The source obtains this uniformity by bounding the error by the
stated norms times a common nonnegative random error, which permits measurable choices of the
test. The common represented event is then chosen for the countable collection of sources and tests
used in this proof. The countable core encodes limits and density classes after the uniform bounds
have been established.


3     Gaussian estimates at the bottom edge scales
The observation below reveals noisy information about leading spin projections. We control the
variance of a bounded spin function left unresolved by that observation by Cp−2 times its heat-bath
energy, with a small remainder, at every scale for which np3 exceeds a tight threshold. The exponent
2 is important: a fixed relative covariance error at every scale would accumulate to a power loss.
We first extend the Gaussian cap estimates to these scales and then show that the relative error is
integrable with respect to d log p.

3.1   Cap observations and the estimates
Throughout this section the disorder is Gaussian, with the independent GOE diagonal and random-
                                                                −4
ized eigenvector signs specified in Section 2. Set rn = n−1/3+10 , the terminal scale of the source
estimates, and write d = 2I − W . Define, in physical coordinates,

                           Pp = U diag(1{da ≤p2 } )U T ,   Hp = ran Pp ,
                           Bp = U diag((p2 − da )+ )U T , Wp = W − Bp ,
                                                               √
                           kp = np3 ,                     Mp = np.

    We use one change of probability law, always conditional on a fixed spectrum. Under the ordinary
law U is Haar, and Qµ is the joint law of the spin and its physical observations at the resulting
W . With probability reference measures in the partition functions, set L◦ (U ) = Zcube (W )/Zsp (W ).
Haar averaging gives EU L◦ = 1. Under P, first choose U with Haar density L◦ , and then use Qµ
given W ; write E for its expectation. The spherical identity of [22, Lemma 2.5 and Equation (2.8)]
gives
                LawP (U T X) = µsp in spectral coordinates,       EU (L◦ − 1)2 = o(1)            (3.5)
on its retained eigenvalue events. Consequently the spectral observation path (U T hp )p under P

The notation Lp (h) suppresses its dependence on U : in a Haar average the spectral field U T h is held
fixed, while Lp (h) still varies with U . At every such field EU Lp (h) = 1. To check the likelihood, the
observation density is a common Gaussian factor times the posterior partition function divided by
the original one, and L◦ cancels the original partition functions. In particular, for every nonnegative

    Every radius, tolerance, and finite multiplicative enlargement of a scale interval is fixed before
the constants are chosen. Increasing K by a fixed factor supplies any such enlargement at the lower
endpoint. A finite number of further requests for cap estimates may be imposed by intersecting
their events, with a smaller failure probability assigned to each. This preserves the constants and
exponent of any variance estimate already obtained.

Corollary 3.5 (Growth of the limiting spectral rates). For GOE-almost every g, and every b with
b + g1 > 0, there are finite positive constants cg,b , Cg,b such that

                         cg,b (1 + i)2/3 ≤ gi + b ≤ Cg,b (1 + i)2/3   (i ≥ 1).                  (3.35)

They can be chosen measurably when b is a measurable admissible function of g.

Proof. Fix errors in Lemma 3.2 for which the number of points with di ≤ p2 is between cnp3 and
Cnp3 , and apply it at the countable scales np3 = 2j K. For any finite list, finite edge-coordinate
convergence transfers the inequalities, with slightly enlarged constants, to counts of limiting points
below (2j K)2/3 . A fixed small buffer in the endpoints may be used; the limiting process has no
point at any of these countably many fixed endpoints. Increase the finite list and use continuity of
probability for decreasing events. The uniform finite-n event shows that all the limiting inequalities
hold with probability arbitrarily close to one when K is large. Letting that failure probability tend
to zero shows that almost surely, after a finite threshold,

                                   cL3/2 ≤ #{i : gi ≤ L} ≤ CL3/2

on dyadic L, and hence on all large L by monotonicity. Invert these inequalities, add b, and adjust
the bounds for the finitely many remaining positive numbers gi + b. The least integer threshold and
rational bounds give measurable choices.

3.4   Location and covariance of the observation path
The uniform cap event controls external fields. We now locate the random field produced by the
Gibbs observation. A shrinking annulus will give an averaged covariance estimate; a fixed annulus
gives the stronger exponential tail needed in Proposition 3.1.

3.5   The covariance iteration and exact-scale variance
The Frobenius estimate says that most directions have a small covariance error on average over a
scale interval. To control the largest positive error, compare a child cap to a larger parent cap. The
radial deficit puts every positive error in the tangent space of the parent field; the unused Haar
rotation then dilutes it in the smaller child space.

                                                  26
   Use the fixed small γ > 0 above. Let q be a child scale and p a parent scale with x = q/p ∈ [γ, 2γ],

4    Warming at the critical time scale
The first step toward a quench limit is to control the finite law after any positive multiple of
Tn = n2/3 , uniformly in its initial distribution. The edge estimates of Proposition 3.1 give the
required control at the smallest observation scales. We turn them into dissipation estimates by
measuring how much relative entropy survives between two observations. Larger entropy levels are
treated by the observation path to a product measure from [20, Section 19].
    For a probability ρ on the cube, write

                                                 d(ρPsTn )
                                         wsρ =             ,          s ≥ 0.
                                                    dµ
Thus ws = wsνn for the uniform initial law used elsewhere in the paper. Write

                                                              Pt (σ, τ )
                                               pt (σ, τ ) =
                                                               µ(τ )

for the heat kernel relative to µ. We use log+ z = max{log z, 0} for z > 0, with z(log+ z)m = 0 at
z = 0. For a probability π and a nonnegative integrable function z, write

                                    Entπ (z) = π[z log z] − π[z] log π[z],

with 0 log 0 = 0 and Entπ (0) = 0. For a probability ν ≪ π, let D(ν∥π) = Entπ ( dν/ dπ), and set
this relative entropy to +∞ otherwise.

Theorem 4.1 (Warming from arbitrary initial laws). Suppose that the couplings are Gaussian. Fix
ϑ > 0, an integer m ≥ 1, and 0 < ξ < 1/100. There are measurable disorder events Gn , a constant
C < ∞, and deterministic εn ↓ 0 such that lim inf n→∞ P(Gn ) ≥ 1 − ϑ, and the following inequalities
hold on Gn , for all sufficiently large n. The constant is independent of the initial law ρ, which may




                                                        32
be chosen after the disorder.
                   Entµ (wsρ ) ≤ C(1 + s−1.54 ),                          s > 0,      for every probability ρ,
                                                                                                          (4.1)
         ∥PsTn ∥L1 (µ)→L∞ (µ) = max psTn (σ, τ ) ≤ C exp(Cs−1.54 ), s ≥ εn ,                    (4.2)
                                  σ,τ
                         1/m                     
  1 + µ wsρ (log+ wsρ )m       ≤ C 1 + s−1/(2/3−ξ) ,
       
                                                                    s > 0, for every probability ρ.
                                                                                                          (4.3)
The events and constants may depend on the displayed fixed parameters. In particular the order m
is fixed before n tends to infinity.
   The distinction between (4.1) and (4.2) is useful. Entropy is controlled at every positive time;
the stronger kernel estimate begins at a deterministic time tending to zero in the rescaled units.
The log-moment estimate supplies a different form of control at the very short times used later in
the replacement argument.

4.1     A restricted dissipation profile
Recall I(z) = E(z, log z), with the extended value at zeros. On a two-point edge, the inequality
                         (a2 − b2 )(log a2 − log b2 ) ≥ 4(a − b)2 ,       a, b > 0,
follows by integrating 1/u between a and b and applying Cauchy–Schwarz. Summing it in the
reversible edge representation gives
                                       I(f 2 ) ≥ 4E(f ).                             (4.4)
The inequality extends to nonnegative f by decreasing positive regularization.
    The next profile concerns a bounded function whose square tilt retains a definite fraction of the
logarithm of its inverse mass. All its inequalities hold simultaneously in the function.

Here a threshold, a positive exponent, and constants are tight in the following precise sense. For
each ϑ > 0 they can be fixed, independently of f , on disorder events of limiting lower probability at

   The modified profile yields entropy dissipation and the higher logarithmic moments after the
reduction by level sets. The classical profile yields a support profile and hence the heat kernel bound.
We prove the profiles in three ranges: L ≤ nδ by the argument with two observations; nδ < L ≤ λn
by the proportional branch of the source path; and λn ≤ L ≤ Clev n by the product endpoint and
the estimate for a reweighted mean below, for a small fixed λ chosen later.

Lemma 4.6 (Retention at order-n entropy). Fix 0 < λ < Clev < ∞, CE < ∞, and 0 < ω < 1. On
Gaussian disorder events of probability tending to one, every test (4.5) with λn ≤ L ≤ Clev n and

                                              E(f )
                                                    ≤ CE n1−ω                                          (4.31)
                                               N
has an observation path to a product posterior for which Hend ≥ cλ,Clev n. The path and disorder
events are chosen independently of f .

Proof. We use the path and the field tests in [20, Section 19.6]; we verify that its entropy argument
remains valid under (4.31). On source disorder events independent of the initial spin law, the calcu-
lation around Equation (19.20) there, using its Lemma 17.4, Equation (17.12), and Proposition 17.5,
gives the following early field-test bound. On a fixed interval [t∗ , tb ] of small positive scalar precision,
the diagnostic and stability failures have integrated probability o(1), uniformly under every initial
spin law. This follows there by conditioning on the spin: the centered Gaussian-noise diagnostics
have integrated failure o(1), the additional spin shift has normalized norm O(t), and the Gaussian
low-band small-ball estimate is uniform in its center. No factor eL is used for this interval. From
tb until the interaction coefficient reaches a fixed globally controlled range, the buffered stability
test fails anywhere on the path with probability at most e−an under the ordinary law Qµ , for a
fixed a > 0. This is the later field bound in [20, Lemma 17.3, Section 19.4, and Section 19.6 after
Equation (19.21)]. The word diagnostics and stability constants on the retained regions are those of
Lemma 4.5.
    Here is the order of the fixed choices in that construction. Choose first a large scalar endpoint
tend and a small interaction coefficient below which covariance is globally bounded. Choose tb > 0
for the small-positive-field estimate, and obtain the buffer and rate a for the later test. The norm
bound and the fixed total duration give constants Cjump and Caft for the jump loss divided by t∗ n
and the loss after failure divided by n, uniformly when 1 − β ′ ≤ t∗ /3. Choose a target drift budget
η1 > 0 so that
                                         3η1 (1 + Caft /a) < λ/4.
Next choose 0 < t∗ ≪ tb with Cjump t∗ ≤ η1 , and finally 1 − β ′ > 0 sufficiently small relative to these
choices, including 1 − β ′ ≤ t∗ /3. Start with precision

                                          B∗ = t∗ I + (1 − β ′ )W,

which is positive when 1 − β ′ is small enough. Increase scalar precision to tend I + (1 − β ′ )W , and
then traverse
                       Bv = (tend + 4v)I + (1 − β ′ + v)W,       0 ≤ v ≤ β′.
The latter rate 4I + W is positive on the norm event. The endpoint is (tend + 4β ′ )I + W , so its
posterior is a product. Before the globally controlled portion, the posterior interaction coefficient is
either β ′ < 1 or in a fixed compact subinterval of (0, 1). Reduce the available diagonal enlargement
in (4.26) to 0 < η < (β ′ )−1 − 1. Once these stability margins and Clead are fixed, choose the mean
accuracy ϵ in Lemma 4.5 so that its leading error, integrated over the bounded path and converted

                                                     44
to a quadratic mean bound, is less than η1 . Then fix its residual tolerance, selection depth, finite
word diagnostics and constant Cϵ , and finally take n large.

                                          √

The fixed choices above make this less than λn/4. The initial entropy is at least L/2 ≥ λn/2, hence

Proof of Proposition 4.2. Fix Clev , ξ and a desired disorder confidence. Apply Lemma 4.3 with
κ < min{ξ, 5/3 − 1.662}. After fixing its M , choose its δ > 0 as in that proof. For L ≤ nδ , (4.8)
and (4.4) imply both claimed inequalities.
    For nδ < L ≤ λn, use the proportional branch of [20, Equation (19.16) and Section 19.4]. It
gives, at the product endpoint,
                                       Hend ≥ cL(L/n)(2+2ϵ)/3 .                             (4.33)

                                                   45
Here ϵ > 0 is any fixed sufficiently small tolerance, and λ > 0 is chosen afterward. We justify
the exact√ test class being used. The starting dimension in that source is max{kmin , c0 L}, with
kmin ≍ log n. It equals c0 L throughout the present range for large n. The proportional part of
its initial jump estimate is the pure-noise argument reproduced in Lemma 4.3; it requires only the
entropy at least L/2 and density at most eL . The source’s support condition is used in its other,
minimum-dimension branch. Its subsequent propagation uses the likelihood and conditional-entropy
tails in [20, Equation (19.8)] and the ordinary path tests. These also require only the same density
and entropy bounds. The continuation through its finite remaining scales uses those likelihood tails
and conditional Pinsker bounds, then globally bounded covariance, and so preserves this test class.
Thus (4.33) applies to (4.5) without a support restriction.
     Since L ≥ nδ ≫ log n, comparison with the first bound of (4.25) gives

                                E(f )
                           Tn         ≥ cn−2ϵ/3 L7/6+2ϵ/3 ≥ cL7/6−(2ϵ/3)(1/δ−1) .
                                 N
The additive O(n−3 ) is smaller than (4.33). The second product bound similarly gives

                                I(f 2 )
                           Tn           ≥ cn−2ϵ/3 L5/3+2ϵ/3 ≥ cL5/3−(2ϵ/3)(1/δ−1) .
                                 N
Choose ϵ after δ so that
                                 2ϵ
                                    (1/δ − 1) < min{ξ, 7/6 − 1.162}.
                                 3
Then choose λ below the finitely many fixed rates and shift buffers in that source argument. This
proves the profiles in the moderate range.
   Finally let λn ≤ L ≤ Clev n. If the classical bound were false, then E(f )/N ≤ Cn1.162−2/3 . This
meets (4.31) for a fixed positive ω, whereas the first product bound would give

                                 Hend ≤ Cn1/2+1.162−2/3 + O(n−3 ) = o(n),

contrary to Lemma 4.6. If the modified bound were false, (4.4) would instead give E(f )/N ≤ Cn1−ξ ,
so that lemma applies with ω = ξ. The second product bound would give Hend ≤ Cn1−ξ = o(n),
again a contradiction.
    All the choices are uniform in f . More explicitly, the variance exponent and M are fixed
first, then the small δ, then the sharp cap and moderate-entropy tolerances, then λ and the fixed
parameters of the order-n path. The finite word length in that last path is fixed after its mean
accuracy. The final threshold K0 and n are chosen last. Intersecting this finite collection of disorder
events preserves arbitrarily high limiting probability. This proves the proposition.

Proof of Theorem 4.1. Fix the parameters in the theorem and intersect the finitely many events

    The three conclusions (4.1)–(4.3) depend only on the off-diagonal couplings. Let Gnaug be the
final event in the augmented Gaussian space, and put

                                  Gnoff = {Woff : P(Gnaug | Woff ) > 0} .

Then P(Gnoff ) ≥ P(Gnaug ). For fixed off-diagonal couplings the three inequalities are deterministic,
so their validity on an augmented realization in Gnaug descends to Gnoff . Taking this as the theorem
event proves the stated disorder quantifiers.

5    Positive-time Gaussian limits and the uniform start
Two inputs give the positive-time edge evolution. For bounded cylinder sources, the finite Markov
resolvent contraction bounds the optimizers used in the stationary relaxed-energy theorem. A
variational argument then gives the required strong semigroup convergence. The warming kernel
bound controls the evolved densities, whose weak limits inherit that evolution. Warming also
controls the total mass in a growing block of edge modes. A rotation argument distributes that
mass among individual modes and retains information specific to the uniform start.
    Throughout this section we use Gaussian couplings and the completed GOE matrix of Section 2,
writing Λ = diag(λ1 , . . . , λn ). Write wn,s = d(νn PsTn )/dµ when the system size needs emphasis.
We use the represented-subsequence convention of that section and denote the probability and
expectation on a representation by P    b and E.
                                               b

                                                    51
Proposition 5.1 (Positive-time Gaussian subsequential limits). Every sequence of Gaussian system
sizes tending to infinity has a further represented subsequence on which the following assertions
hold almost surely. The edge coordinates converge to g, and there is a measurable random family
hs ∈ L2 (Πg ), s > 0, with the following properties. The family may at this stage depend on the
represented data in addition to g.
    There is a finite random C ≥ 1, independent of s, t, such that

        0 ≤ hs ≤ K(s) := C exp(Cs−77/50 ),            Πg [hs ] = 1,             hs+t = Stg hs ,    s, t > 0,   (5.1)

where Stg = exp(−tc∗ Hg ). The map s 7→ hs is continuous in L2 (Πg ). For each t > 0, Stg has a
nonnegative symmetric kernel ktg relative to Πg satisfying

                       0 ≤ ktg (x, y) ≤ K(t)    for Πg ⊗ Πg -almost every (x, y).                              (5.2)

The constant 77/50 is the exponent 1.54 in Theorem 4.1.
   On this subsequence,

                An,J −→ Ag      in Cloc ((0, ∞)),             Bn,J −→ Bh         in Cloc ((0, ∞)2 ),           (5.3)

where
                                                          Πg [hs xa Stg xa ].
                                                    X
                                      Bh (s, t) =                                                              (5.4)
                                                    a≥1

The series in (5.4) converges locally uniformly and is continuous. Finally,

             ∥hs − 1∥L2 (Πg ) −→ 0,       Bh (s, ·) −→ Ag (·)         in Cloc ((0, ∞))       (s → ∞).          (5.5)

Proof. We first pass the semigroups on bounded sources, then construct the densities, and finally
justify the sum over all edge coordinates.

Varying L2 spaces. Choose a countable family C of smooth compact cylinders with this approx-
imation property: every smooth compact cylinder is approximated uniformly, together with its
first derivatives, by elements of C supported in one fixed compact coordinate set. Rational smooth
approximations on rational boxes give such a family. The stated approximation and the definition
of the closed form make C dense in its form norm, and also dense in L2 (Πg ). For linear tests put
V = spanQ (C ∪ {1}). This is a countable family of bounded smooth cylinders, dense in L1 (Πg ) and
L2 (Πg ). If ϕ ∈ V depends on the first d coordinates, let ϕn = ϕ(x1 , . . . , xd ) on the finite cube.
     The static cylinder and moment convergence of [22, Corollary 3.3] gives

                                  µ[ϕn ψn ] −→ Πg [ϕψ]           (ϕ, ψ ∈ V).                                   (5.6)

On sequences bounded in L2 (µ), the weak convergence of Section 2 can be tested on this countable
family: the pairings with every ϕ ∈ V converge to the corresponding pairings with f if and only if the
pairings with every smooth compact cylinder do. To pass from V to an arbitrary smooth compact
cylinder, approximate it uniformly on a common support by C, and use the uniform L2 bound to
control the pairing error in both spaces. The converse follows from the same approximation, with
cutoffs tending to one for the constant test. A sequence bounded in L2 (µ) has a weakly convergent
subsequence: diagonal extraction of its pairings defines a bounded linear functional on the cylinder
span by (5.6), and the Riesz representation theorem supplies its L2 (Πg ) representative. The same
argument gives
                                    ∥f ∥L2 (Πg ) ≤ lim inf ∥fn ∥L2 (µ) .
                                                          n


                                                      52
As in Section 2, the convergence is strong when the norms also converge. For such a sequence,

                              lim ∥fn − ϕn ∥2L2 (µ) = ∥f − ϕ∥2L2 (Πg )         (ϕ ∈ C).            (5.7)
                               n

Consequently strong convergence is equivalent to approximation, in both spaces, by the same
cylinders. In particular, the pairing of an L2 -bounded weakly convergent sequence with a strongly
convergent sequence converges.

Resolvents from bounded optimizers.                    Set

                    qn = Tn E,          An = Tn Hn ,         q = c∗ Πg [|∇ · |2 ],   A = c∗ Hg .

For ϕ ∈ C and rational z > 0, consider

                                   un = (z + An )−1 ϕn ,          u = (z + A)−1 ϕ.

The finite Markov property gives ∥un ∥∞ ≤ z −1 ∥ϕ∥∞ . The resolvent identity also bounds ∥un ∥2
and qn (un ) uniformly. Thus these particular optimizers satisfy the polynomial supremum bound
as well as the norm and energy bounds in the relaxed-energy theorem [22, Theorem 6.8]. That
theorem applies to disorder-dependent tests under the class-uniform quenched convention recorded
in Section 2. This proof invokes it only for the countably specified optimizer sequences, one for each
ϕ ∈ C and rational z > 0, on their common represented event. Its lower bound therefore gives

                       lim inf qn (un ) ≥ q(U )        whenever un has weak limit U .              (5.8)
                          n

   Here is the variational passage, including the norm conclusion. Let

               vn = sup{2⟨ϕn , f ⟩µ − z∥f ∥22 − qn (f )} = ⟨ϕn , un ⟩µ = z∥un ∥22 + qn (un ),
                      f

and define v by the analogous supremum in L2 (Πg ). Take an optimizer subsequence attaining the

                              lim sup vn ≤ 2⟨ϕ, U ⟩Πg − z∥U ∥22 − q(U ) ≤ v.
                                    n

For the reverse inequality, take F ∈ C. The recovery part of [22, Theorem 6.8] supplies, for each fixed
recovery index j, polynomially bounded finite functions strongly converging to F , whose energies
converge to q(F ) in the order n → ∞, then j → ∞. Testing the variational supremum with these
functions in exactly that order gives

                                    lim inf vn ≥ 2⟨ϕ, F ⟩Πg − z∥F ∥22 − q(F ).
                                        n

The form density of C allows the supremum over F , proving vn → v. Only the countably many
sources in C and rational z are used on the common source event. The limiting variational problem
is strictly concave, so every weak optimizer limit is its unique optimizer u. Moreover,

                                        lim sup z∥un ∥22 ≤ v − q(u) = z∥u∥22 .
                                            n

Together with norm lower semicontinuity this proves strong convergence of un to u. This is the
bounded-source version of the variational argument in [22, proof of Lemma 7.2].

                                                         53
    The bound ∥(z + An )−1 ∥2→2 ≤ z −1 and (5.7) now extend this resolvent convergence to every

Densities and the limiting kernel. On the represented sequence, Theorem 4.1, with fixed
admissible auxiliary parameters, gives a finite random C and numbers δn ↓ 0 such that ∥PsTn ∥1→∞ ≤
K(s) for all s ≥ δn . Since wn,0 is a nonnegative density and the semigroup is self-adjoint,

                 0 ≤ wn,s ≤ K(s),        µ[wn,s ] = 1,         ∥wn,s ∥22 ≤ K(s)    (s ≥ δn ).    (5.10)

Include the pairings of these densities with V at each positive rational s in the representation. Every
limit functional Ls obeys

                 |Ls (ϕ)| ≤ K(s)Πg [|ϕ|],       Ls (ϕ) ≥ −K(s)Πg [ϕ− ],           Ls (1) = 1.

Here ϕ− = max{−ϕ, 0}. The bound follows from (5.10) and static convergence also for the continuous
cylinders |ϕ| and ϕ− . It extends Ls to L1 (Πg ). The second inequality proves positivity of the
extension: if cylinders ϕj converge in L1 to f ≥ 0, then Πg [ϕ−  j ] ≤ ∥ϕj − f ∥1 → 0. The extension is
therefore represented by a density 0 ≤ hs ≤ K(s) of mass one. In particular ∥hs ∥22 ≤ K(s). This
also justifies normalization if only compact cylinders were initially retained: their cutoffs tending to
one have complements bounded by K(s) times the corresponding static probabilities. Measurability
can be seen concretely. Take nested finite cylinder partitions which generate the product Borel sets
and whose cell boundaries have zero Πg measure; rational coordinate boxes have this property by
the cylinder densities of [22, Proposition 2.3]. The value Ls (1A ) for each cell is a measurable limit
of its values on fixed smooth approximations to 1A , which can in turn be approximated by C. On
each cell A put the ratio Ls (1A )/Πg (A), with value zero if the denominator is zero. These ratios are
measurable, bounded by K(s), and are the conditional expectations of hs on the nested partitions.
Their bounded martingale limit gives a measurable representative of hs .
    For positive rational s, t, reversibility and (5.9) imply for every ϕ ∈ C

                            Πg [hs+t ϕ] = lim µ[wn,s PtTn ϕn ] = Πg [hs Stg ϕ].
                                            n

We used the weak–strong pairing rule; the wn,s are weakly convergent and bounded in L2 . Hence
hs+t = Stg hs . The closed cylinder-gradient form is a Dirichlet form: smooth normal contractions
obey its contraction inequality, and closure preserves it. Constants belong to the form and have

                                                    54
zero energy, by [22, Proposition 5.1]. Thus Stg is Markov and preserves one. For a real s > 0, choose
                                  g
a rational q < s and set hs = Ss−q   hq . The rational relation makes this definition independent of
q. Strong continuity of the semigroup proves L2 continuity in s > 0, as well as the relation for all
s, t > 0. Positivity and mass one persist. Taking rationals q ↑ s in the density bound, using L2
convergence and an almost-everywhere subsequence, proves hs ≤ K(s). For deterministic dyadic
rationals qj (s) → s, every cylinder integral at s is the limit of its measurable values at qj (s). It is
therefore jointly measurable in the environment and s. The same partition construction therefore
gives jointly measurable representatives for the real-time family.
     The finite convergence against cylinders also holds along every sequence sn → s > 0. Indeed, for
s ≥ δ > 0, the identity wn,s = P(s−δ/2)Tn wn,δ/2 and spectral calculus give

                                                2p

Approximation of s by a rational time, followed by the weak convergence there and the L2 continuity

    For completeness we obtain the kernel needed to sum coordinates. For nonnegative smooth
compact cylinders ϕ, ψ, the finite kernel bound yields

                                       0 ≤ µ[ϕn PtTn ψn ] ≤ K(t)µ[ϕn ]µ[ψn ].

Passing to the limit using (5.9), and then approximating indicators in L2 (Πg ), gives the same
inequality with Πg [ϕStg ψ] in place of the left side. Define on rectangles the nonnegative functional

                                               1A×B 7−→ Πg [1A Stg 1B ].

For a simple function on rectangles use common disjoint partitions of the two factors. The preceding
rectangle inequality then bounds the functional on every nonnegative such function by K(t) times its
L1 (Πg ⊗ Πg ) norm. It extends to a positive bounded functional on that L1 space, so has a density
ktg with 0 ≤ ktg ≤ K(t). Self-adjointness makes the density symmetric. Its marginals are Πg , since
Stg 1 = 1. This proves (5.2); it does not require a joint pointwise version of the kernels for all t.

Correlation limits and relaxation. Orthogonality of the eigenframe gives the exact finite
identities
                          n

The preceding two-copy reduction and (5.12) give uniform finite correlation tails on every compact

uniformly on compact positive time sets. The same Cauchy–Schwarz argument with (5.15) therefore
gives the limiting uniform tail control.
    It remains to pass each fixed summand. Static convergence of the mixed pairings xa ϕ and
the second moment x2a makes the finite coordinate xa strongly convergent to the limiting xa . If
sn → s > 0, then wn,sn xa is weakly convergent to hs xa . To see this, test first against a cylinder
times a smooth cutoff of xa at height R, and use the convergence after (5.11), extended from C

The fixed second moments are bounded and converge, so this error vanishes uniformly as R → ∞.
Also ∥wn,sn xa ∥2 ≤ K(δ)∥xa ∥2 . The weak–strong pairing rule and (5.9) now give, whenever sn →
s > 0 and tn → t > 0,
                                µ[wn,sn xa Ptn Tn xa ] −→ Πg [hs xa Stg xa ].
The same argument without wn,sn gives the fixed stationary summand. Each limiting summand is
continuous. For t ≥ δ > 0, the kernel bound gives

                        ∥Stg xa ∥∞ ≤ K(δ)Πg [|xa |],             ∥xa Stg xa ∥2 ≤ K(δ)∥xa ∥22 .

Thus L2 continuity of hs gives continuity in s, uniformly for t in positive compact intervals. Strong
continuity of Stg xa , paired with hs xa ∈ L2 (Πg ), gives continuity in t. The uniform tail bound
proves the asserted continuity and local uniform convergence of the full series. It also proves the
two locally uniform finite limits in (5.3): failure of uniform convergence on a compact set would

                                                           57
provide a sequence of time points with a convergent subsequence, contradicting the fixed-summand
convergence, the uniform tails, and continuity of the limiting sum.

                           |Πg [(hs − 1)xa Stg xa ]| ≤ K(δ)∥xa ∥22 ∥hs − 1∥2 −→ 0.

The correlation tails are uniform also for s ≥ s0 , since ∥hs ∥∞ ≤ K(s0 ). Passing first through a finite
sum and then letting its length tend to infinity proves (5.5).

    The preceding proposition does not yet identify the family as a function of g. The following
estimate retains the information from the finite uniform initialization that will make this identification
possible.

6    Selection of the entrance law
Proposition 5.1 gives positive-time density families for the edge semigroup, but an extracted
family might still depend on randomness beyond the edge points. The uniform-start estimate of
Proposition 5.2 is averaged over the represented environment. We first prove fixed-g uniqueness
from a sequential escape criterion and derive the zero-coordinate boundary from that criterion. We
then extract a qualifying sequence for almost every represented environment and obtain a family
measurable in g.
    Fix g in a common Borel full-measure subset of the edge set chosen in Proposition 5.2 on
which the conclusions about the conditioned law and the closed gradient form in Items 1 and 3 of
Proposition 2.1 hold with their stated source versions. The rate, finiteness, and positivity conclusions
of Proposition 5.2 hold on this set as well. In this section write

                    ri = gi + b(g),        D = Dg (b(g)),      Π = Πg ,          H = Hg .


                                                     63
Thus D > 0, and there are constants 0 < cg < Cg < ∞ such that

                             cg (1 + i)2/3 ≤ ri ≤ Cg (1 + i)2/3    (i ≥ 1).                      (6.1)

Constants with a subscript g below may be enlarged or decreased and depend only on this fixed
realization, unless other dependencies are displayed. Recall the finite, strictly positive random
variable
                                           X x2

                                               2ri
                                               i≥1

Its finiteness follows from the uniform coordinate moment bound Π[x2i ] ≤ Cg /ri and i ri−2 < ∞;
                                                                                        P
its positivity follows from the absolute continuity of a one-coordinate marginal. These properties of
Π are part of the conditioned edge law [22, Proposition 2.3].

Theorem 6.1 (Entrance selection). For every g as above, there is at most one family (hs )s>0 of
probability densities with respect to Πg with the following properties:

   1. hs ≥ 0, Πg [hs ] = 1, and sups0 ≤s≤s1 ∥hs ∥L∞ (Πg ) < ∞ whenever 0 < s0 < s1 < ∞;

   2. hs+t = Stg hs in L2 (Πg ) for s, t > 0, where Stg = exp(−tc∗ Hg );

   3. there is a sequence sk ↓ 0 such that, for every i ≥ 1 and ε > 0,
                                           h                i

                                                     i



Any such family satisfies

                      hs Πg =⇒ δ0      on RN with its product topology, as s ↓ 0.                (6.4)

For almost every g such a family exists. It can be chosen measurably in g, as a family of L2 (Πg )
density classes, and with jointly measurable density representatives in (g, s, x). Every represented
Gaussian limit in Proposition 5.1 is this family almost surely.

    The hypothesis (6.3) describes information retained from the finite uniform initialization. Differ-
ent candidate families may use different sequences in Item 3. The zero-coordinate limit (6.4) is a
consequence of that hypothesis and holds as s decreases to zero through all positive times.

Lemma 6.2 (Angular realization). Let w be a bounded probability density with respect to Π. There
is a continuous process X(t) = (Xi (t))i≥1 on RN , with law (Set w)Π at time t, and independent
standard Brownian motions (Wi )i≥1 in its filtration, with the following property. Almost surely
R(X(t)) > 0 for all t ≥ 0. With R(t) = R(X(t)), Θi (t) = θi (X(t)), and r(t) = r(X(t)), one has
                                  √
                                                                            

                                                                           

All identities hold simultaneously for i ≥ 1. The Brownian increments after zero are independent of
the initial sigma field.

Proof. The closed form of H is Eg (F, G) = Π[∇F · ∇G]. Its domain is the closure of the smooth
compact cylinder functions, contains the constants and all coordinates, and satisfies ∇xi = ei
[22, Proposition 5.1]. Smooth contractions on the core extend by closure, so this is a Dirichlet
form. The same approximation, with smooth clipping to preserve a common bound, gives the chain
and bounded product rules. In particular the gradient vanishes almost everywhere on a set on
which the function is constant: apply the chain rule to smooth truncations in successively smaller
neighborhoods of the constant, and use closedness. These rules show strong locality.
    Here are the topological hypotheses for the diffusion theorem. The state space RN is Polish in the
product topology. The smooth compact cylinder core consists of continuous functions and contains
a countable family separating its points. To obtain a compact nest, for each coordinate choose a
smooth function vj , equal to one outside a sufficiently large coordinate interval and zero on a smaller
interval, with ∥vj ∥Eg ,1 ≤ ϵ2−j . Such a choice is possible: coordinate tails have Pvanishing Π-mass,
while a transition of increasing width makes the gradient norm vanish. The sum j vj converges in
the form norm and is at least one on the complement of a compact product box. The definition of
1-capacity therefore bounds that complement’s capacity by ϵ2 . Taking ϵ ↓ 0 and enlarging successive
boxes gives a compact nest. The form is thus quasi-regular. Since 1 has zero energy, its semigroup
fixes 1; the associated process has infinite lifetime under Π, and hence under wΠ.
    The diffusion theorem and locality criterion for quasi-regular symmetric Dirichlet forms now give
a continuous associated process [18, Theorems IV.3.5 and V.1.11]. Its Fukushima decomposition is
supplied by [18, Theorem VI.2.5]. In particular, the coordinate martingales M i are continuous and
have brackets                               Z t
                                 i   j
                             ⟨M , M ⟩t = 2      ∇xi · ∇xj (X(v)) dv = 2δij t.
                                              0
The bracket identity is the energy-measure identity for the form, given by [13, Theorem 5.2.3 and
Equation (5.2.34)] after the quasi-regular regularization of [18, Chapter VI]. To apply its bounded



                                                   65
formula, first take a bounded smooth clip uN = cN (xi ). For every bounded form-domain test f , the
chain and product rules give

                             2Eg (uN f, uN ) − Eg (u2N , f ) = 2Π[f |c′N (xi )|2 ].

Choose the clips with |c′N | ≤ 1 and cN (xi ) → xi in form norm. Passing to the limit in the energy
measures and polarizing gives the displayed coordinate √brackets. The multidimensional Lévy
characterization, for every finite set of indices, gives M i = 2Wi with the asserted countable family
of Brownian motions. Brownian motion here is relative to the filtration of the process, so its future
increments are independent of the initial sigma field. The usual form exceptional set has Π-measure
zero; it can be discarded for wΠ. These facts also give continuous coordinate paths.
    We verify the generator used for θi . The finite-coordinate density formula for Π, on any finite
set J, is                                             
                           qJ c D − j∈J (x2j − rj−1 ) Y  r 1/2
                                     P
                                                               j         2
                                                                   e−rj xj /2 .                  (6.7)
                                       q(D)                   2π
                                                            j∈J

Here q is the density of the full centered Gaussian square sum, and qJ c is the density after the
coordinates in J are deleted [22, Proposition 2.3]. Both densities exist, q(D) > 0, and qJ c has
bounded derivatives of any prescribed finite order. For the last assertion, leave sufficiently many fixed
indices outside J; the product of their characteristic-function moduli is bounded by (1+4t2 rj−2 )−1/4 ,
                                                                                       Q
which is integrable even after multiplication by the prescribed power of |t|. Fourier inversion then
gives the assertion. In particular (6.7) is bounded by a constant times the product Gaussian density.
    The set R = 0 is polar. Indeed a smooth cutoff equal to one on {R ≤ ϵ}, zero on {R ≥ 2ϵ}, and
with derivative O(ϵ−1 ), has squared form norm O(ϵm + ϵm−2 ), by (6.7). Letting ϵ ↓ 0 proves zero
capacity. A diffusion starting outside its exceptional set never hits a polar set, which proves the
stated pathwise positivity of R.
    For a cylinder test whose index set J contains {1, . . . , m, i}, integrate by parts in (6.7). The
derivativePof the factor qJ c is radial in the J coordinates. Its pairing with the gradient of θi vanishes,
because j∈J xj ∂j θi = 0. Consequently the nonpositive generator on this function is
                                                        X
                                        Lθi = ∆θi −           rj xj ∂j θi .
                                                        j∈J

This integration by parts is legitimate across R = 0. More explicitly, cut off at R = ϵ and at
large finite-coordinate radius. The function and its first two derivatives near zero are bounded by
Ci (1 + |xi |)(1 + R−3 ). The products with derivatives of the scale-ϵ cutoff satisfy the same bound on
R ≍ ϵ. Their squared integrals near zero vanish for m = 12 > 6. Terms with a derivative on qJ c are
bounded by the same Gaussian integrals with polynomial factors, since its first derivative is bounded.
Gaussian tails handle the outer cutoff. Thus the cutoffs converge in the form norm, proving θi
belongs to the form domain, and the weak generator pairing passes to the limit. The displayed
Lθi is in L2 (Π) by the same bounds. Extending the pairing from the core shows θi ∈ Dom(H) and
Hθi = −Lθi .

    We next isolate the part of the equation that remembers the initial state. It becomes independent

Lemma
P          6.6 (Escaping seeds). Suppose that, along a sequence of initial states possibly random,
  i pi = 1  and pi → 0 in probability for every fixed i. Then

               Ay −→ K      in probability, uniformly on compact subintervals of (0, 1].            (6.29)

For each fixed t > 0, the seed stochastic term Gyq (t) tends to zero in L2 , uniformly over all adapted q
satisfying |q(v)| ≤ C0 v −1/4 .

Proof. By (6.16), 2ri Ki → K uniformly on a fixed compact positive     P interval, and these functions
are uniformly bounded there. In the convex combination Ay = i pi 2ri Ki , the contribution of a
fixed finite set tends to zero in probability and the contribution of its complement is uniformly close
to K. This proves (6.29).
    For the stochastic assertion, its squared norm is at most
                                     Z t         "                    #
                                                  X
                                 CC0     v −1/2 E    pi ri Ki (t − v)2 dv.
                                      0            i

For every v < t, (6.15) implies ri Ki (t − v)2 → 0, whereas (6.17) bounds it uniformly. Escape of pi
makes the expression in brackets tend to zero in probability, and its uniform bound gives convergence
of its expectation. Dominated convergence against v −1/2 proves the assertion.

6.3    Coupling entrances
We now prove the deterministic uniqueness and boundary assertions of Theorem 6.1. Work in unit
time (6.5). Let two proposed families be given, with starting sequences uℓ,k ↓ 0, ℓ = 1, 2. Start the
diffusion of Lemma 6.2 from e huℓℓ,k Π, and construct (Qℓ , fℓ , Yℓ,i , yℓ,i ) as above. Their seed weights
escape in probability by (6.3) and (6.11). The constants below depend on g, and are uniform in k
and in the two families.
    We may use the same Brownian motions for the two diffusions in elapsed time even though
strong existence has not been asserted. Here is the coupling argument. Each joint law of the
Brownian path W and all its other continuous path variables is a probability on a standard Borel


                                                       73
path space. Disintegrate it over W , and, given one Brownian path, sample the two other path
variables independently from these conditional laws. Brownian motion remains Brownian in the
joint filtration. To see this, if Φℓ is a bounded observable of the past of process ℓ through time t,
independence of future Brownian increments in that process implies

                      E[Φℓ | W on [0, 1]] = E[Φℓ | W on [0, t]]          almost surely.

Test this equality first against products of functions of the Brownian past and future increments,
and then use a monotone class argument. For a product Φ1 Φ2 , conditional independence in the
coupling makes its conditional expectation the product of two functions of the Brownian past. A
second monotone class argument proves independence of future increments from the joint past.
Completion and right-continuous augmentation preserve this property. Finally, stochastic integrals
are limits in probability of adapted simple sums. Their laws in each marginal are unchanged by the
coupling, and they are the same integrals in this joint Brownian filtration. Thus all the preceding
stochastic identities hold on the coupling.

Stops with uniform failure bounds.             Choose C0 so large that
                                                         √
                                       C02 > 1 + sup sup t Ay (t),
                                                  0<t≤1 y

where the supremum is over the normalized seeds in (6.11); it is finite by the bound on Ay
                                                                     0                   −1/4 ). Let

ηk ↓ 0, as slow as necessary, such that with probability tending to one both solutions satisfy
                                 √
                                    t Ayℓ (t) ≥ 2c  (ηk ≤ t ≤ 1/2).                            (6.30)

in L2 (Π) implies L1 convergence. The couplings of their two elapsed-time marginals are tight on

                                                   76
6.4   Measurability and Gaussian identification
Return now to the theorem’s critical time s = u/c∗ , so that the evolution is by Stg = e−tc∗ Hg .
It remains to obtain the family from g itself and to check existence. We give the measure-
theoretic passage, since the escape estimate in Proposition 5.2 initially averages over the represented
environment as well as x.
    On any represented Gaussian limiting space write µs = hs Πg . At rational s > 0 these probability
measures are measurably encoded by their integrals against a countable convergence-determining
collection of bounded continuous cylinders. Equivalently

                                     µ = (µs )s∈Q>0 ∈ P(RN )Q>0

is a random element of a standard Borel space. The asserted measurability follows from that
encoding: a countable separating collection embeds P(RN ) injectively and measurably in a countable
product, and its inverse on the image is measurable. By the convention in Proposition 5.2, the
escape quotient is jointly Borel on RN × RN . For each fixed i and ε > 0, its threshold event is a
Borel subset of this parameter/state space, so the evaluation of that event under µs is a measurable
function of (g, µs ).
    The annealed escape estimate permits a deterministic rational sequence sk ↓ 0 for which, almost
surely in the represented data, (6.3) holds for every i, ε. To see this choose sk < min{2−k , sk−1 /2}
rational, successively, so that
                                   X
                                 E    µsk {x2i /Mg (x) > 1/k} ≤ 2−k .
                                   i≤k

Proposition 5.2 allows this choice. Tonelli’s theorem shows that the sum of the displayed random
quantities over k is finite almost surely. For fixed i, ε > 0, eventually i ≤ k and 1/k < ε; the
corresponding probability in (6.3) consequently tends to zero. The positive-time semigroup and
boundedness properties hold on the same full-measure set by Proposition 5.1.
    The finite-coordinate formula (6.7) makes g 7→ Πg a measurable probability kernel. The edge
semigroup is also measurable in g in its countable varying-L2 encoding. To see the precise claim
needed here, take a countable smooth cylinder core. Its Gram entries Πg [ϕi ϕj ] and form entries
Πg [∇ϕi · ∇ϕj ] are measurable. For λ > 0 the variational formula

                       ⟨ϕ, (λ + Hg )−1 ϕ⟩Πg = sup{2Πg [ϕv] − λΠg [v 2 ] − Eg (v)},
                                                v

where v ranges over the rational span of that core, proves measurability of the resolvent pairings;
polarization gives mixed pairings. Choose the countable core sequence linearly independent as
cylinder functions, with dense real span. The positive finite-coordinate densities make every finite
Gram matrix positive definite. The coefficients of the orthogonal projection of an encoded L2 vector
on a finite core span are its cylinder pairings multiplied by the inverse Gram matrix; they are
measurable. These projections converge in L2 , proving the corresponding measurability for arbitrary
encoded vectors. Continuous functional calculus then gives measurability of Stg in the same encoding,
jointly for t > 0; one can approximate e−tc∗ Hg by continuous functions of (1 + Hg )−1 , and use strong
continuity in t. This is also the countable-core construction in the functional identification proof for
the stationary edge law [22, proof of Proposition 9.2].
    The normalization, boundedness, evolution, and escape conditions are properties of the countable
code together with g. Normalization and bounded domination by Πg can be checked on a countable
cylinder algebra, with an integer bound on each rational compact time interval. Evolution can
be checked against the same tests at rational times; it then extends to real times by strong L2

                                                    77
continuity. The preceding core construction makes the semigroup pairings measurable in g. Thus
these conditions, together with the displayed countable escape tests, define a measurable full set of
codes on which the deterministic theorem applies.
    Disintegrate the law of (g, µ) over g. For almost every g, two independent conditional samples of
µ both extend to the positive-time families just considered and both pass (6.3). Fixed-g uniqueness
therefore makes these samples equal. A probability measure on a standard Borel space whose
two independent draws agree almost surely is a point mass (apply the assertion to a countable
separating collection of Borel sets). Thus the conditional law of µ given g is a point mass almost
surely. Its point depends measurably on g: the map z 7→ δz is a measurable injection into the space
of probability measures and has a measurable inverse on its image. This constructs measurable µgs
for rational s, and establishes existence.
    We spell out the extension to density classes and real times. A measurable Radon–Nikodym
theorem applied to the kernels µgq ≪ Πg gives measurable representatives hgq (x) for rational q.
    For real s > 0, choose any rational q < s and set
                                                    g
                                             hgs = Ss−q hgq .                                    (6.37)

The rational evolution relation makes this independent of q. Choosing q = q(s) by a fixed measurable
rational rule and using the semigroup measurability established above shows that the density classes
in (6.37) are measurable in (g, s). The corresponding measures form a measurable kernel; the
measurable Radon–Nikodym theorem once more gives jointly measurable representatives hgs (x).
They are the L2 -continuous extension furnished by the semigroup, with the stated normalization
and local boundedness.
     For a second extracted Gaussian law, condition its code over the same GOE law of g. Its
conditional samples pass the criterion on some sequence by the same argument, so fixed-g unique-
ness makes its point mass the one already constructed, outside a common null set. Thus every
subsequential Gaussian limit is determined by g. The locally uniform correlation convergence and
relaxation in Proposition 5.1 now hold for this hg , and the subsequence criterion proves the asserted
joint Gaussian convergence. Finally, undoing (6.5) returns the evolution hgs+t = e−tc∗ Hg hgs . The only
time substitution in this section was the temporary variable u = c∗ s; the coefficient in Stg remains
c∗ .


7    Smooth truncation of clock histories
The replacement of the coupling variables requires bounds for clock paths whose duration is of order
T = n2/3 . We obtain them by capping transition densities on every scale of a binary time tree. This
section constructs the caps, proves that they discard negligible Gaussian mass, and records the
conditional path laws to which the score estimates will apply.
    All times in Sections 7–8 are in site-clock units. A stationary query at lag t is one path of
length tT started from µ. A quench query (s, t) is one path started from νn , with consecutive legs
of lengths sT and tT . Fix a compact set of query times. Every complete leg then has length in
[cT, CT ], where 0 < c < C < ∞ are fixed. We use Taylor orders at most K = 24. The constant
β > 0 will be chosen sufficiently small after K. The notation a ≲β b means a ≤ C0 nC1 β b, with
C1 depending at most on K and the fixed query set, but not on β. Fixed logarithmic factors can
be included in this convention. Moment orders and the precision of probability estimates may be
increased without changing C1 ; their constants and the lower bound on n may change. For a vector




                                                   78
indexed by En = {{i, j} : 1 ≤ i < j ≤ n}, put
                                                         !1/p
                                          1 X
                             |v|p =       n
                                             |ve |p            ,     |v|∞ = max |ve |.
                                                                                e∈En
                                           2   e∈En


7.1    A static support valid for every interaction matrix

Write θij = Jij / n. In entry replacement, an order-k derivative in θe is multiplied by n−k/2 ;

match through order three. The averaged fourth derivative of the truncated trace tests must
therefore tend to zero, while higher derivatives may grow within the remaining negative power of n.
The estimates below are organized around this fourth-order requirement; K = 24 supplies a global
remainder bound.

Proof. Let ψ be smooth and nondecreasing, zero on (−∞, 2], with 0 ≤ ψ ′ ≤ 1 and ψ ′ = 1 on [3, ∞).

WN (1 − WN ) times a bounded polynomial in WN , which proves the first part of (7.6). If WN ≥ n−L ,
then aN ≤ L log n + O(1) < (log n)2 ; hence l/N < 2 for large n. In this region aN is affine, and its
slope in l is (log n)2 /N . The same derivative formula proves (7.7). It also proves the second part
                                                                  2
of (7.6) for l/N ≤ 2. In the remaining region WN ≤ e−(log n) , so the first global bound with its
logistic factor gives the second bound there as well, since N is polynomial in n. Finally the two
logarithms are bounded by C(1 + |aN (l/N )|). Since ψ is Lipschitz and (log n)2 /N ≤ 1 for large n,
this proves (7.8).

    Let pd (x, y) = Pd (x, y)/µ(y) be the true transition density. Define the pre-density k̄I , the survival
factor wI , and the post-density kI , all relative to the normalized probability µ, by

             k̄I (x, y) = p|I| (x, y)                                                    if I is a leaf,
                          X

                           z
            wI (x, y) = WNI (log k̄I (x, y)),         kI (x, y) = k̄I (x, y)wI (x, y).

All these numbers are positive. In particular, the logarithms are defined. The transition kI (x, y)µ(y)
is a subprobability. At a fixed leg we may equivalently sample an ordinary marked heat-bath path
and retain it with the product of its wI (Xa , Xb ) over all nodes. Apply checks at their right endpoints,
ordering equal right endpoints from the smallest interval to the largest. The equivalence follows
inductively from the Markov property and (7.9).

Lemma 7.3 (Deterministic kernel consequences). For every node I of length d,

                        0 < kI (x, y) ≤ k̄I (x, y) ≤ pd (x, y),       kI (x, y) ≤ eCN (d) .                (7.10)

Both kI and k̄I are symmetric in (x, y). If an interval of length h lies inside a checked tree, with h
                                                                                        ′
larger than a fixed constant, the weighted transition across it has density at most eC N (h) after all
weights other than one contained complete node are discarded. The same assertion holds when the
top check of the tree is omitted or is a failure factor 1 − wI .

Proof. Positivity and domination by pd follow by induction and the semigroup property of Pd . The
cap is (7.5). At a split the two children have equal lengths and use the same rule, so their kernels
are identical. The convolution of this symmetric kernel with itself is symmetric; multiplication by
the endpoint function WN (log k̄I ) preserves symmetry. This starts from detailed balance of pd .
     Every subinterval of length h larger than a fixed constant contains a tree node J of length
at least c1 h. One may require J to be a proper descendant at the cost of reducing c1 . Keep the
checks in J and discard the other weights, all of which lie in [0, 1]. The remaining transition is an
ordinary kernel, followed by kJ , followed by an ordinary kernel. Since ordinary kernels preserve µ
                                                    ′
and constants, its density is at most eCN (|J|) ≤ eC N (h) . A discarded failure or top factor also lies in
[0, 1].

and over its initial probability on the cube.

7.3    Tested histories and their conditional interiors
We record precisely which changes of path measure will be used. An ordinary marked path includes
the time, site, and new sign at every ring, including a resampling that keeps the current sign. Its
law on a time interval I from x is denoted by PIx .

Definition 7.5 (Tested path law). A checked history is one of the following subprobabilities, with
initial probability either µ or the uniform cube probability:

  (i) an entire query path with every survival factor in its legs;

 (ii) the prefix through a specified first failing check F , with all preceding survival factors and the
      factor 1 − wF at that check.

The check order is the one following (7.9); the choice of F and all tree shapes is fixed independently
of the path and of e. In case (ii), the maximal checked intervals of the prefix form a forest: the last is
F , whose descendants survive, and every preceding root and its descendants survive. Designate the
initial state, all leg boundaries, and all boundaries of these maximal roots as its external endpoints,
retaining only boundaries present in a stopped prefix. Multiply the history by a nonnegative function
Φ of these endpoints with ∥Φ∥∞ ≤ nM+ , where M+ is fixed and Φ is the same for every e ∈ En .
When the resulting mass mΦ is at least n−M− for a fixed M− , its normalization is a tested path
law, denoted by QΦ .
    Products of a fixed number of such histories use independent ordinary paths and a separate
external test on each history. Signed tests are decomposed into positive and negative parts separately.
A statement that holds with arbitrarily high inverse-polynomial probability means that for every
fixed A its exception has QΦ probability at most n−A for all sufficiently large n, uniformly in the
fixed choices above and in disorder satisfying (7.2).

                                                        83
     There are at most polynomially many nodes. In the last, possibly partial, leg of a first-failure
history, its preceding maximal roots are the earlier siblings encountered in the binary subdivision
leading to F . In their chronological order their lengths are nonincreasing and the last such length is
at least |F |. Complete earlier legs have lengths comparable to T . These facts also show that earlier
maximal roots have lengths at least a fixed multiple of later ones, with the multiple depending
only on the query set. The density of QΦ relative to the ordinary forward law with the same initial
probability is at most nM+ +M− ; future endpoints are not revealed in that forward law’s natural
filtration.                            Q
     For a node I = [a, b], write VI = J⊊I wJ (X∂J ), with the product over its proper descendants.
The normalized interior law with boundary (x, y) is

the top check. A top survival, failure, or omitted check is constant after its boundary is fixed.

Lemma 7.7 (Overlaps along a checked history). With arbitrarily high inverse-polynomial probability
under a tested path law, simultaneously for times in any one of its checked root intervals,

   |ρ2 (X(t1 ), X(t2 ))| ≲β (1 + m)−1/2 ,    |ρ4 (X(t1 ), X(t2 ), X(t3 ), X(t4 ))| ≲β (1 + m)−3/4 .   (7.14)

In each assertion the times are increasingly ordered, and m is the minimum of their consecutive
gaps and of the gap from the root’s start to t1 . The assertion includes a failing root. It also holds
for the full history obtained by each of a fixed finite collection of interior resamplings in Lemma 7.6,
separately within each such history. All times in an overlap are taken from that one history.

Proof. First use deterministic times with m larger than a fixed constant. Inside each gap choose a
contained proper checked node of length comparable to m. They are disjoint. Dropping all other
weights and applying Lemma 7.3 shows that the joint law of the observed states, given any initial
state, is bounded by their product µ law times exp(C2 N (m)). This estimate counts the retained
weights without conditioning on survival. Apply (7.2) at a dyadic scale comparable to m, first to all
relevant pairs, and then to the quartet on the event that its pairs obey the same threshold. The
ratios a2 (m)/N (m) = n2β and a4 (m)/N (m) = n7β tend to infinity. Their exponents are at least
a positive power of n, so they pay the cap and any polynomial test density with any prescribed
inverse-polynomial precision. A vacuous threshold or bounded m gives the bound directly from
|ρj | ≤ 1.
     Use a time mesh of sufficiently small inverse-polynomial spacing. The probability that any mesh
cell has two rings is arbitrarily small after paying the polynomial test density: under ordinary paths
the total clock rate is n, and the union bound is O(n2 T ) times the spacing. A single ring changes
either overlap by at most 2/n per affected time. This is smaller than the displayed thresholds, whose
smallest quartet value has order n−1/2+10β . A union over the polynomially many grid tuples proves
the simultaneous assertion. A resampled interior has the same tested marginal by Lemma 7.6; a
union proves the last claim for any fixed number of copies.

Lemma 7.8 (Coarse coefficients and moments). On the size support in (7.2), for all nodes,
boundaries and e, and 1 ≤ l ≤ K,
                              k̄ u      ku        wu         1 − wIu

The exponent CK is independent of β in a fixed sufficiently small range and of any later number
of conditional copies. Absolute fixed-order moments of clock counts and raw path coefficients are
polynomial under tested laws and their resampled marginals. Conditional on an arbitrary boundary
pair of one node, the same moments under Cx,y    I  are bounded by a polynomial (with exponent allowed
to depend on the moment order). Finally, conditional on arbitrary designated external endpoints,
the absolute normalized coefficient through total order K of an entire checked or first-failure history
is bounded by nCK , after enlarging CK . Thus its unnormalized coefficient with any nonnegative
external endpoint test held fixed under the interaction perturbation is bounded by nCK times the
test’s base mass, however small that mass is.

Proof. The size support gives e |θe | ≤ Cn4 , hence minx µ(x) ≥ e−Cn . By Cauchy–Schwarz, each
                                  P
absolute one-site field is at most Cn7/2 . On a leaf, prescribe one ring of each site in successive
subintervals of length 1/n, with its sign set to the desired endpoint, and no other rings. The
                                                                                                      7/2
clock event has probability e−n|I| n−n , and each prescribed mark has probability at least 12 e−Cn .
                                                          5
Thus every leaf transition probability is at least e−Cn , uniformly in its endpoints. Its logarithmic
density has absolute value at most Cn5 . The last assertion of Lemma 7.2 and the kernel recursion
propagate this property up the tree: composing two positive kernels costs a fixed multiple of the
previous logarithmic bound plus a polynomial, and applying WN has the same property. The depth
is O(log n), so all these absolute logarithms remain polynomial, with a fixed exponent. In particular
                     C
